 [vote?id=50635288&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50635288]
brookst [user?id=brookst] 20 hours ago [item?id=50635288] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50634969] | next [#50637078] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It’s a weird industry. It’s in high demand, but consumers are satisficed as long as they are physically transported, so there’s almost no room for differentiation, so profits are near zero. And costs are volatile with weather and fuel prices, but there’s always going to be another airline willing to roll the dice on costs not increasing, so if you charge a little more to hedge risk, you sell no tickets, and your fixed costs don’t change.

reply [reply?id=50635288&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350635288]

Zero margins are what bring about innovation. Someone decides they have a new way of doing things that can be cheaper than what others have done. Airlines have shown this over and over again. My take is that this is the reason why flying is so affordable. It's affordable enough that most adults in the US can fly to the places they want to visit. It's not perfect, but over time, it seems to work.

WheelsAtLarge [user?id=WheelsAtLarge] 16 hours ago [item?id=50637287] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50637210] | prev [#50638786] | next [#50638805] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Competition from new players is what usually changes things. These days startups are what R&D used to be. A new, innovative startup comes along and changes everything. Many companies, even very profitable ones, consider R&D a drain on profits, so they cut it altogether or reduce it to a minimum.

 [vote?id=50638805&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50638805]
phil21 [user?id=phil21] 12 hours ago [item?id=50638805] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50635472] | prev [#50637071] | next [#50636683] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Regulation is the only reason airlines are so competitive. Regulation is so effective in this area of industry that safety is very close to as optimal as current human society can provide.
After safety is taken into account, consumers pretty much only care about price. But if safety was a factor I think we would see a wildly different market.
It’s interesting to me that regulation has been so effective in this particular industry, where in most other places (healthcare as an example) you see almost the opposite outcome.

 [vote?id=50638560&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50638560]
labcomputer [user?id=labcomputer] 13 hours ago [item?id=50638560] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50635918] | next [#50636666] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

That's not at all unique to the US.
In fact, it is extremely rare (I can't think of a single example) for a country to allow a foreign airline to sell tickets between two points in that country, unless that flight is a code-share (or some other form of partnership) operated by a domestic airline. Likewise, airlines based in country A are generally not allowed to operate a route between a city in country B and one in country C.
To the extent that foreign airlines from country B are allowed to fly in country A (but only non-stops to cities in B), it is invariably the result of a bilateral treaty that also allows country A's airlines to operate non-stops to cities in country B.
This is one of the reasons that all Emirates flights connect in Dubai, or Iceland Air flights from the US to Europe always have a stopover in Reykjavik.

reply [reply?id=50638560&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350638560]

I just ran a comparison of operating a 737-200 in 1980 vs a 737 Max 8 in 2025. The inflation adjusted hourly operational cost was higher in 1980 and the adjusted ticket price was still cheaper. Plus, in 1980, everyone in economy got a heated meal as a "bonus" and much more generous free luggage affordance.
They're raking in profit with their bean counting. They are not suffering.

 [vote?id=50637657&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50637657]
olalonde [user?id=olalonde] 15 hours ago [item?id=50637657] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50636666] | next [#50637078] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> the adjusted ticket price was still cheaper
The average U.S. domestic round trip was about twice as expensive in 1980 as it is today, after adjusting for inflation. That includes average baggage and change fees.[0]
> raking in profit
Airlines earn about $1.70 in profit per $100 of domestic revenue. Those are pretty thin margins to call "raking in profit".[1]
[0] https://www.airlines.org/dataset/a4a-presentation-industry-r... [https://www.airlines.org/dataset/a4a-presentation-industry-review-and-outlook/]
[1] https://www.bts.gov/newsroom/us-airlines-profited-60-billion... [https://www.bts.gov/newsroom/us-airlines-profited-60-billion-2025-decrease-over-2024]

nostrademons [user?id=nostrademons] 19 hours ago [item?id=50635574] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50634622] | next [#50637171] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Arguably videoconferencing is a better, more efficient technology than air travel. It's far more convenient, takes 0 time, 0 fuel, 0 labor, minimal electricity even, you aren't crammed into a metal box for 6-14 hours, you can do it on-demand, and it's safer than even the airlines' pretty good safety record.
That's an example of software outright avoiding the problem that the physical world creates. The set of business meetings that can be done using Zoom is a non-trivial subset of all business travel. My wife was just on a board of directors meeting for one of her portfolio companies and when I complained about having to manage the kids for her 3-hour 5:30 AM Zoom call she was like "I just saved you a 3-day cross country business trip; you're welcome."

 [vote?id=50636189&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50636189]
nostrademons [user?id=nostrademons] 18 hours ago [item?id=50636189] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50636043] | next [#50637043] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It's a pretty good example of a disruptive innovation. Disruptive innovations are worse than the technologies that they replace, and as such don't fully replace them. However, they're better in one specific dimension (oftentimes cost or convenience), and they're "good enough" for a significant fraction of users. Over time that fraction tends to increase as the disruptive technology itself gets better.
In my wife's case, she's not trying to influence policy, she doesn't need deep relationships with the rest of the board (though she actually has them already, since she's been working in this space for over a decade), she doesn't have particular opinions on who the management team is - she just needs to make sure the company isn't fucked. It's a satisfice vs. maximize thing. The type of things that would actually worry her would be very apparent and not need a whole lot of subtlety to interpret.
Actually AI is another good example of a disruptive technology: for a lot of the things it's being hyped for, it's significantly worse than alternatives, but it only needs to be better in one or two dimensions for a few markets to gain a foothold (but quite possibly not a foothold that's worth $1.5T at this time).

 [vote?id=50634167&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50634167]
otherme123 [user?id=otherme123] 21 hours ago [item?id=50634167] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50633898] | next [#50634365] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

More than competition is that there is close to zero customer preference between company A and company B. There is a lot of competition in the soda market, but client preferences are usually not price sensitive: yes, Cola-X is cheaper, but I love Coca-Cola even at a higher price. Same with sneakers, or clothing, some are "allowed" to have higher margins.
AI labs, the same than airplanes or printing paper: you use Claude because it is slightly better for what you pay. The minute anyone releases a better model or an equal model but minimally cheaper, you switch AI provider. Zero margin, zero brand loyalty, huge capital expenses just to keep afloat.

reply [reply?id=50634167&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350634167]

 [vote?id=50635039&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50635039]
notahacker [user?id=notahacker] 20 hours ago [item?id=50635039] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50634167] | next [#50634569] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The bigger difference is that operating aircraft is extremely capital intensive and most of the other costs are fixed or near-fixed too (like LLMs and unlike sugary drinks). A commercial aircraft has a capital cost of $xxx,xxx a month, generally on multi-year lease terms which means you need it operating as much as possible, not just at the most profitable times... and then operating it sends even more money out of your bank account before you've sold a single ticket. It's also nearly as expensive to fly when it's empty as full, so generally the most profitable way to operate a flight segment involves slashing prices at the margin until you're nearly full and then trying to eke out a profit on upsells or business class. In practice, the better optimized airlines can make 10-20% which isn't bad compared with most retail, but the average margin is dragged down by all the airlines that aren't making anything at all (and what happens when their revenue model gets shocked by the economy and they're stuck with most of their costs)
There actually typically isn't that much competition on individual direct flight segments, but if a particular segment is very profitable and not completely closed off to competition, someone else will happily redeploy their expensive aircraft from a less profitable route to compete with you on it. And yeah, that's where it's also similar to LLMs. There might only be a handful of providers, but everyone compares and any niche that earns outsized profit from one is likely to see at least one other follow. There's a theoretical possibility an LLM company might differentiate itself from the others for a longer period of time which is what all the VC money is chasing, but it's not what's happening so far....

I like the airline/soda comparison because it shows the different nature of the products. Flying through the air to get anywhere in the world is a magic, society-altering product. It’s hard and expensive to run an airline, but if you got one, you’re capturing 99% of the magic. Soda just ain’t that.
LLMs are an incredible, world-altering class of product, but the ergonomics and branding of competing products just matter less. If intelligence is equal, I’m gonna go with the cheaper one.

 [vote?id=50635717&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50635717]
nostrademons [user?id=nostrademons] 19 hours ago [item?id=50635717] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50634478] | next [#50635090] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

There's plusses and minuses to that.
There are plenty of state airlines: Emirates, ITA, Aeroflot, Air New Zealand, and many smaller companies. They usually offer better service than a privatized corporation, because they have a monopoly position within their country.
However, they are usually significantly more expensive than private carriers. Since airline deregulation in the U.S, airfares have fallen 45% adjusted for inflation [1]. And state carriers are usually more expensive than private ones on the same routes: compare Emirates prices vs. any other airline on a route that doesn't connect through Dubai, for example.
Non-profit != cheaper; it just means that the money is directed toward other things, like perks or employee salaries or other nice-to-haves, and that it doesn't face competitive pressure to operate more efficiently.
[1] https://www.econlib.org/library/Enc/AirlineDeregulation.html [https://www.econlib.org/library/Enc/AirlineDeregulation.html]

Lots of airlines are owned by governments and don't make profits. Generally they don't provide better service or cheaper prices than the commercial equivalents though, even if they do sometimes provide a valuable loss-making service on very marginal routes.
Unlike tap water, the commercial airline market does allow for competition and unlike many other services government buying power doesn't really move the needle that much.

Airlines will never be meaningfully profitable because the competition will always drive prices down. There is no technological barrier to entry, only a capital barrier.
AI will be similar to airlines in this respect. Leading labs be damned, most people want to pay for the thing that is good enough to answer basic questions, not solve a math problem we've never heard of.
And, on the rare chance they achieve agi, we're cooked anyways - money won't matter if that happens in the long run. Like nuclear fusion.

mandeepj [user?id=mandeepj] 20 hours ago [item?id=50635105] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50633393] | prev [#50634794] | next [#50633502] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> Air Travel is a transformative technology and very few Airlines make any real money.
It's one of the myths circulating here!
Let's take an example: The average fare from Chicago to Denver is around $300, but let's take $250. A 9-seat row with 30 rows brings the trip revenue to $67,500. However, there are more rows and higher prices for business class. I also didn't account for charges for upgrades, extra luggage, checked luggage, food purchases, etc. All in, the operating cost for an airline for that trip is around $25k.

 [vote?id=50635473&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50635473]
nostrademons [user?id=nostrademons] 19 hours ago [item?id=50635473] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50635105] | prev [#50635241] | next [#50635226] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Then you add ground crew salaries, airport gate fees, promotions, sales & marketing, legal & HR, and all the other stuff required to operate a major corporation.
I just spot-checked financials for UAL/DAL/AAL/LUV/JBLU. They all tell similar stories. Operating margins (this is your profit per flight, the revenue for flying the plane minus the cost of operating that flight divided by revenue) run about 25% for healthy airlines (UAL/DAL), 18% for unhealthy airlines (AAL/LUV/JBLU). Net margins (this is the profit available to shareholders after all expenses including fixed costs have been subtracted out) run about 5-8% for the healthy airlines and are negative for the unhealthy ones. That's why Warren Buffett calls airlines a disaster for the capitalist class. Sure, they run a profit on a per-plane basis: but capitalists have to pay for a lot more than running the plane. The industry is heavily competitive, and any airline that hasn't gone bankrupt recently has heavy capital costs for buying the planes and building out their network that make them uncompetitive vs. other airlines.

Betelbuddy [user?id=Betelbuddy] 20 hours ago [item?id=50635226] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50635105] | prev [#50635473] | next [#50635359] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Not a myth...
Over the last decade (2016–2025), commercial aviation moved 40 billion passengers, cutting real airfares by 37%, and powering 4% of global GDP. On $8 Trillion in revenue...global airlines netted just $42 Billion, roughly $1 per passenger.
With only 7 of 100+ carriers consistently beating their ~8% cost of capital. Aviation creates trillions in value captures almost none for shareholders.
IATA: https://www.iata.org/en/publications/annual-review/ [https://www.iata.org/en/publications/annual-review/]
McKinsey: https://www.mckinsey.com/industries/travel/our-insights/coul... [https://www.mckinsey.com/industries/travel/our-insights/could-aviation-at-last-be-close-to-earning-its-wings]

> The entire world is barely worth a few trillion
Wealth gets created. Global GDP is around $120 trillion annually, in nominal terms.
A century ago, global GDP was around $6 trillion.
Humanity's output increased 30-fold while population only quadrupled. Economic output per person increased 7-fold.
Put differently, we now produce as much economic value in 12 days as produce in an entire year a century ago.

> Most people in today's modern world [...] barely get 5 days off.
Where'd you get that idea? There are 5 countries in the world where you annually get 5 days off or fewer, three of which are currently the USA or formerly administered by the USA (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_minimum_annual_leave_b... [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_minimum_annual_leave_by_country]) and the remaining two are Nauru and Nigeria. With my western glasses I'd say 25-30 days is a normal minimum (on a 5-day workweek) but the first low-income country that comes to mind, Liberia, also has 21 days minimum. Most of Africa seems to have more than that. You're looking at the bottom 3% of countries and some % of their inhabitants will get more than the minimum leave. This is far from what 'most people' experience
It also helps that the risk of starving to death decreased by orders of magnitude at the same time as population increased by orders of magnitude. Vaccinations that we take for granted are widely available worldwide (https://ourworldindata.org/grapher/global-vaccination-covera... [https://ourworldindata.org/grapher/global-vaccination-coverage]) and people don't universally carry a sickness anymore. The median adult in the middle ages had some ailment or other. We tend to now get those sorts of issues after a good amount of healthy adult life and most are solvable or much more manageable (with modern treatment) than they would have been even just one hundred years ago
As for sitting in an office/vehicle instead of plowing fields or hoping to find enough food each day while foraging... you do you
If being (on average) secure and healthy is 'not living', we may have different ideals

 [vote?id=50639512&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50639512]
KellyCriterion [user?id=KellyCriterion] 10 hours ago [item?id=50639512] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50635070] | next [#50635229] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

>believe it's what another person down the road put in their bank
well, this is true:
a) loans are created on "local corebanking level", but if you want to do a transfer to another bank, you need that amount on your central bank's account
b) if a bank would not get any deposits from "another person down the road", then they wouldnt be able to transact with other banks
c) what most people do not understand: A bank does not need deposits to create a loan on your local level in your community bank, a bank needs deposits (which are arriving on the central bank level, from others!) to be able "transfer away" that loan, that was created 1 min ago, to another bank (like getting a loan to buy a house and then paying the money to the seller of the house at another bank)
(Source: Working in this field)

I chose the word "take" because thats what actually happens.
Take the recent data center trend that seems to be happening around the world. Locals do not receive any benefits from a local data center, they only lose.
A few jobs to the local security company do not balance the negatives the majority of thousands receive. If everyone is paying more for property or electricity or any of the other things in life that were cheaper, then they have lost. This is a very simplistic overview but its only a sample, the trends clearly show that AI is not helping.
Big Data is causing all prices to go up, because everyone from AMZN to all the others are watching what people buy and instead of striving for lower prices they are pushing up because they know people need homes, food and fuel.

_Tev [user?id=_Tev] 3 hours ago [item?id=50642865] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50635254] | next [#50635628] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> You can't have infinite growth in a finite system.
Fascinating how often people repeat that fallacy.
https://www.reddit.com/r/explainlikeimfive/comments/17aj24q/... [https://www.reddit.com/r/explainlikeimfive/comments/17aj24q/eli5_how_does_the_sum_of_infinite_terms_turns_to/]
You can argue about how fast the growth has to decrease in order for that finite-sum being possible, but then you are talking about shape of the curve instead of silly "infinite growth" strawman. Even the biggest AI boosters will not claim the exponential growth will go for "infinite time" . . . The entire debate is about the shape of the curve: "Have we hit the S-curve plateau already?". Nothing about infinity.

Here’s a scenario. Anthropic gets to the famed RSI (recursive self improvement), either full, or more likely partial, and they build a model, let’s call it Haiku 6, which has the intelligence of Opus 5.5, but is served at $0.25/Mtok. And they do this at a profit. Then everyone stops using OSS models, or Sol or Gemini, amd switches to Haiku 6. Except for people who consume free inference. Anthropic will then be able to raise bonds at 6%, while OpenAI will raise at CoreWeave levels, 13-16%. If that lasts for one year, OpenAI is out, and Google switches to leasing their datacenters to Anthropic.

 [vote?id=50635586&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50635586]
CoolestBeans [user?id=CoolestBeans] 19 hours ago [item?id=50635586] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50634878] | next [#50636260] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Mail providers have high switching costs and are part of larger office productivity ecosystems which create lock in. AI providers have yet to create this kind of moat despite best efforts. The API, harness, CLI, and filesystem model means switching is just an API key change (or with an API router a click) away. Even locking the API to the harness doesn't mean much if all the work is in the filesystem.

reply [reply?id=50635586&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350635586]

 [vote?id=50639613&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50639613]
KellyCriterion [user?id=KellyCriterion] 10 hours ago [item?id=50639613] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50635586] | next [#50635744] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

>AI providers have yet to create this kind of moat despite best efforts
they do already! Companies are building processes completely with Claude etc., the lock-in is already ongoing: But compared to email, where you could easily setup your own, you wont easily & happily setup / maintain / support your own LLM instance (yes, edgecases exist here as well)
For most people, running their own LLM infra is just as appealing to run your own email - therefore: Outsourcing.
And thats where the big guys will be the behemoth, while some smaller tech-shps may exist and they will serve whatever specific/niche customers.

 [vote?id=50636260&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50636260]
Scaled [user?id=Scaled] 18 hours ago [item?id=50636260] | root [#50629503] | parent [#50634878] | prev [#50635586] | next [#50634492] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Mail is outsourced because you're basically forced to do so if you want to land in inbox instead of spam. Unless you have a sufficiently large volume of transactional mail, most companies just don't have enough volume for Gmail/etc to bother building reputation on your IP and domain. It's not outsourced out of convenience, but by force. It's a strong moat, and given Google/Microsoft/etc all sell email services, is likely not coincedtal.
AI's moat is different in that it's not being forced by an external entity. You can buy some expensive hardware and deploy open models and get decent (but not frontier) quality. It's usable and will get better over time. Whereas for mail, the external providers basically force you to use a major platform for practical usage -- and that will never improve over time.

reply [reply?id=50636260&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350636260]

The obligations are agreed in costs only, not a specific number of GPUs/racks, so they are worth ~$900b today or tomorrow because the hardware is only being purchased at time of deployment (give or take 12 months). They won't change in value much until deployed, and then once deployed, will depreciate fast.

 [vote?id=50635732&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50635732]
ralphington [user?id=ralphington] 19 hours ago [item?id=50635732] | prev [#50629503] | next [#50628548] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

We've built a model agnostic continuous development system using about $10k/wk ai budget. The system has a component that measures AI models, both frontier and open weight, for value independently for orchestration, investigation, implementation, and review. The output is to production systems: wall clock and error rate matter the most and the budget is moderately elastic.
Our model cost breakdown shifts as new models come out, but the latest breakdown by company is ~ 65% anthropic, 20% openai, 10% Google, and 5% open weights.
It's hard for me to take seriously the comments that say GLM is good enough for real work. It has its place and it can be measured.
We're greedy and would rather pocket savings, but for our systems, the above is the most profitable arrangement, and it'll naturally shift as the model landscape evolves.

seizethecheese [user?id=seizethecheese] 17 hours ago [item?id=50637044] | root [#50635732] | parent [#50636899] | next [#50638880] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Good enough is actually better if it's faster! We have an open source battle system (https://github.com/bottomless/agent-duel [https://github.com/bottomless/agent-duel]) where people can vote early, and DeepSeek v4.1 Flash is holding its own in the leaderboard ( https://agentduel.com/leaderboard [https://agentduel.com/leaderboard] ) in spite of costing $0.07 vs the top models costing ~$2. The reason is that it's really fast (2min cost per task vs Opus closer to 3 and Fable closer to 4).
But the problem with local AI is that it's actually not faster.

> The LocalAI community seem to have this opinion that "Good Enough" to fumble through a task with numerous reworks and repeat bug fixing, is equivalent to Fabel 5.2.
That is a fine straw man you have there, but nobody has this opinion about equivalence between Fable and anything that runs locally.

If you’re going to argue against something, it’s good practice to characterise it properly.

 [vote?id=50639670&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50639670]
dofm [user?id=dofm] 10 hours ago [item?id=50639670] | root [#50635732] | parent [#50637139] | next [#50638039] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

If you buy a Mac Studio now, and run it for your work tasks for a year, at the end of it you still have a Mac Studio, which you can probably sell at next to zero loss (if you had done this last year you would actually have been in profit!)
I am not going to get into what your definition of “prime time” is, because it seems clear to me that for me specifically, local AI is doing the work I need it to do. That is my own opinion. You have your opinion and that is fine.
I just want to observe that a year’s money sunk into cloud AI is gone at the end of the year. A Mac Studio will likely not have depreciated.
The same is, as far as I can see, true of all on-premises inference hardware. You need the up front money but for the next year or three there is unlikely to be that much depreciation, and you are buying hardware that can do other jobs, including run the development environment hosting you will need to buy anyway.
Even after year three, unless you have worked it to death, you will have an asset that can be sold.

Can I ask what real work you are doing, with what models, and quants?? Im have Qwen 3.8 Flash next quant 4 running on my strix halo device, standing right next to my Anthropic and OpenAI subscriptions.
I trust 3.8FN to do exactly 0 production work, it's a toy that tends to immolate nearly every codebase it touches. I REALLY want localAI to be good. I think one day soon in the next 18-24 months, it will have reached a tipping point where every dev, IT, security, or technical worker will be issued a 256GB or 512GB ram laptop running localAI models for 75% of their work, and using subscriptions for only the hardest stuff. But I just cannot see how anyone in their right mind can justify spending $4000-$8000 per dev on hardware to run localAI models for real work at the quality open weigh models offer today. And I just cant see how many western aligned businesses will send their IP through services like open router or to Chinese infrastructure. In fact I know a large number of businesses are legally unable to via regulations. Finance/Banking, Health Care, Defense, Government, etc all cannot do that and remain compliant.

Breakfast: 2 slices of wholewheat, lightly toasted. 1 egg, over easy. 2 shots of espresso, no sugar, no cream.
Lunch: skip it or get overweight
Supper: boiled turkey breast, lightly peppered, with a dollop of ranch. 50g of peas. 2 light beers.
This amounts to a perfect mix of 300g of protein, 3750 kcal of carbs and about 8 grams of fat per day. I won't take anyone who disagrees with the above diet seriously, since I personally battle-tested it.

 [vote?id=50634250&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50634250]
simplyluke [user?id=simplyluke] 21 hours ago [item?id=50634250] | parent [#50630227] | prev [#50633504] | next [#50633642] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Net income at IPO, time to first profitable quarter. Caveat that there's a lot of creative accounting in tech, and it's gotten more creative with AI companies.
Amazon (1997): -$5.78 million - first profitable quarter Q4 '01
Netflix (2002): -$21 million - first profitable quarter Q2 '03
Tesla (2010): -$154 million - first profitable quarter Q1 '13
Facebook (2012): +$1 billion at IPO
Anthropic (2026): -$100 billion through sept 2026
One of these things is not like the other. Anthropic's financials on serving existing models are great, their debt to stay relevant going forward is atrocious, and their competitive moat depends entirely on continuing (and accelerating) that spending. Amazon, netflix, tesla, and facebook did not need to spend exponentially more money to become profitable.

reply [reply?id=50634250&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350634250]

manquer [user?id=manquer] 17 hours ago [item?id=50636775] | root [#50630227] | parent [#50634250] | next [#50637152] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> competitive moat depends entirely on continuing (and accelerating) that spending
It is bit worse than that, there is no guarantee that more spending will translate to a defendable moat in the future.
Meaning they could spend the $1T in commits and still not able to create a substantially better model than current gen and their frontier peers or open weights ones are able to keep pace without the same budgets.
Even if they did make significant improvements in the next few generations, distillation and other techniques could help competition stay close enough at much cheaper cost - training being expensive for first developer.

_Tev [user?id=_Tev] 2 hours ago [item?id=50642995] | root [#50630227] | parent [#50637816] | prev [#50639708] | next [#50628208] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> I suppose it hinges on whether or not you expect AI to accelerate GDP growth.
That is actually very questionable.
Will AI continue to improve? Sure.
Will it improve in an accelerating fashion? Eh, whatever, sure.
Will laid off people due to AI continue to consume as before? Nope.
Will somehow the profit of companies be redistributed among people for the aggregate consumption to hold? . . . No?
It is questionable whether AI will even be in the short term positive for GDP. Technology disruptions lead to bigger efficiencies eventually, but there might be a pretty big dip along the way.

laserDinosaur [user?id=laserDinosaur] 1 day ago [item?id=50628532] | prev [#50628208] | next [#50628261] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Anthropics business model makes me think of how things would have played out if the internet was invented by a single private company. Sure anyone can host their own ISP, but the cost of it still prohibits most regular users. All websites are hosted in the gigantic data centers that one or two companies own, and all traffic goes through them. What would that company be worth? What would the long term prospects look like as more and more ISP's start to enter the field? Especially as costs come down for more competitors. In some ways I guess that's kind of the trajectory of AOL.

ndiddy [user?id=ndiddy] 21 hours ago [item?id=50633956] | root [#50629921] | parent [#50633114] | next [#50630401] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I would not buy Anthropic stock myself at the current price, but the compute obligation thing doesn't really make sense to me. It's not like having $800 billion in debt. It's "we want to pay for compute at a guaranteed price for x years". Even if Anthropic stops being a competitive company, as long as the market for compute doesn't collapse and they're able to resell it, they won't be going bankrupt. Look at X.AI for one example. They spent a ton of money building datacenters but nobody wanted their Grok models. Now they make their money by selling compute to other AI providers rather than selling inference.

grogers [user?id=grogers] 20 hours ago [item?id=50634957] | root [#50629921] | parent [#50633956] | next [#50634315] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Yes, they will be able to resell excess compute capacity at some price, but possibly not at a price that keeps them afloat. The scenario where Anthropic ends up not using their negotiated compute seems likely to be correlated with data center capacity overall outstripping demand.

 [vote?id=50635278&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50635278]
ndiddy [user?id=ndiddy] 20 hours ago [item?id=50635278] | root [#50629921] | parent [#50634957] | next [#50634315] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I can think of lots of scenarios where Anthropic wouldn't be able to use all of its negotiated compute but compute is still valuable. For example, if commodity open-source models are able to do most commercially viable tasks and the frontier models are only valuable for advanced research. Most companies would switch over to the open source models, but there would still be a need for compute to run them on.
Even in a different scenario where Anthropic can't profitably resell their compute, and they end up using less compute than they anticipated, it's still not the end of the world. Looking at Anthropic's IPO prospectus, $120B of their compute comes from X.AI, which can be cancelled with 90 days' notice. If they still need to shed more compute, ~$85B comes from Nscale and Lambda, which is only paid out when they deliver the compute. They won't be able to deliver without tens of billions of dollars in capital, so Anthropic won't have to worry if the market collapses. If that's still not enough, ~$280B comes from Amazon, Microsoft, and Google, all of which are major shareholders in Anthropic. They have a material interest in Anthropic remaining a viable company, so they would likely be able to work out a deal (i.e. offering equity in exchange for relief from some of their payment obligations).

reply [reply?id=50635278&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350635278]

 [vote?id=50632320&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50632320]
surgical_fire [user?id=surgical_fire] 23 hours ago [item?id=50632320] | root [#50629921] | parent [#50630625] | next [#50639032] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Because I think that regardless of how useful AI can be, Anthropic is in a ruinous path financially.
Just to survive, Anthropic needs to generate revenues in the several hundred billions. I am not talking about profitability, I am just talking about survival.
When you see people here going about how much they can do on their $20 or $200 plan, those are extremely unprofitable to Anthropic. They would need to get everyone on API pricing, or massively increase subscription prices.
This is untenable in a world where open models can provide similar capabilities to their flagship models for the kind of work most people do.
Lay on top of this a scenario where interest rates are on the rise and there is a possibly a challenging economic environment in the next couple of years (oil prices very expensive due to wars, possibly food shortages due to El Nino).
Note here that this is completely divorced from AI as a technology, any considerations if we are in a financial bubble or not, etc.

 [vote?id=50628157&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628157]
JumpCrisscross [user?id=JumpCrisscross] 1 day ago [item?id=50628157] | parent [#50628123] | next [#50628406] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> bummer you can't buy options the day of the IPO
Former options market maker here. There are good reasons for this.
One, you don’t know volatility. That makes dynamic hedging properly a capital-intensive exercise that could wind up dominating volume over price-setting traders.
Two, IPOs are already risky. Adding a route for retail to leverage that is a recipe for disaster.
There is also the issue of having to police lock-up evasion. Minor, but material. (It also means your natural-hedging market is largely prohibited from participating.)

reply [reply?id=50628157&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350628157]

LeoUnderwood [user?id=LeoUnderwood] 16 hours ago [item?id=50637198] | prev [#50628798] | next [#50639395] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Two deal terms seem relevant to the price action. Insiders were allowed to sell up to 316 million shares starting on Day 1, instead of the usual 6-month lockup, so early supply was much larger than a typical IPO. And the banks selling the stock also lent Anthropic $15B, which is worth keeping in mind when reading the underwriters' coverage.

 [vote?id=50629514&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50629514]
jerf [user?id=jerf] 1 day ago [item?id=50629514] | prev [#50629999] | next [#50628101] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

This is extremely common, and even as someone who frequently discusses the "AI bubble", this doesn't mean a heck of a lot. There are a lot of structural forces that apply to a newly IPO'd stock both formal (various lockup periods ending and supply coming online, Anthropic's schedule of this is particularly complicated) and informal (that day one pop is manufactured by design). You'll find if you try to trade on this general pattern that you've mostly been beaten to it; the options will have this pattern already priced in, you won't be able to borrow stock to short for any reasonable price, etc. The only thing you can really do is understand that day one may not be the best day to get in, in general, for any IPO.
I popped the question into my friendly neighborhood AI as to whether there have been any major IPOs where the IPO price ended up serving as the floor for the stock, and it's a short list, though with some major names in it. Microsoft (1986), Nvidia (1999), Google (2004), Visa (2008), and in the 2020s, Airbnb, Snowflake, Zoom (2019, though arguably helped a lot by a Certain Event), Reddit, and more-or-less Arm. Amazon and Tesla are near-misses. But there's been a lot of IPOs in that time frame. Don't know how complete the AI coverage is, but plenty of very successful businesses have dipped below their IPO. It's the common case.
On the plus side, while we may complain that the ever-increasing delay between the companies being founded and their IPO means that venture capital is capturing more and more of the gains, at least for IPOs themselves, the "little guy" who couldn't get in on the initial offer almost always get offered the chance to get in at lower than that price, so that's at least some tiny break for the little guy.

reply [reply?id=50629514&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350629514]

 [vote?id=50628101&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628101]
littlecranky67 [user?id=littlecranky67] 1 day ago [item?id=50628101] | prev [#50629514] | next [#50634228] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

They have no future business model. Open-weight models and specialized distilations can be hosted in anyones datacenter where fierce competition will bring down prices. The current problem is the hardware scarcity and that the few big frontier providers snatch all the hardware at inflated prices while at the same time offer subsidized tokens below cost. Once the market evens out that scarcity, the 10-15% gap of the latest and greatest frontier model won't be able to justify the prices and valuations.

 [vote?id=50628643&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628643]
ryandvm [user?id=ryandvm] 1 day ago [item?id=50628643] | parent [#50628101] | next [#50628844] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

They have a business model - it's just so disturbing they can't say it out loud.
It's the same model Google and Facebook use. They're going to advertise so effectively it will make your head spin. Today's advertisers use signals and tracking and they get pretty close to guessing what you're in the market for.
The new generation of AI advertisers are going to know every bump and ridge and your demographic profile because of all the weird questions you like to ask your AI. They'll know your prejudices and your political opinions, your interests and your icks, but most importantly - your cognitive biases.
Then they're going to take it to the next level. The AI companies are going to have their models actively nudging you in the direction of their advertising clients. You're going to be subjected to subliminal advertising campaigns so subtle and natural that you won't have any idea that it even happened. Anthropic/OpenAI/Google/Facebook are going to have their models working on you for months to buy that new Range Rover or take that trip or ask your doctor about Fukitol because of course that's what "a smart guy like you" would do (according to your trusted model).
It's dark. And it's going to be wildly successful.

 [vote?id=50628771&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628771]
program_whiz [user?id=program_whiz] 1 day ago [item?id=50628771] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628643] | prev [#50634561] | next [#50628748] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

unfortunately for Anthropic, those other sites have a fair amount of "lock in" that Claude lacks. There still isn't a great (free, fast, scaled) google alternative. Social media sites have network effects. Anyone can make these types of sites, but having the data and the users is hard.
Meanwhile in AI land, there are numerous alternatives that are just about as good. There is no switching cost, and no network effect (it doesn't get worse for me even if I'm the only user).
In their case, enshittifying their app for profit won't work, as the only thing keeping customers around is quality. If they enshittify, then they lose to competitors who got a late-mover advantage, have a nearly as capable model, and are serving up with no ads or restrictions for $0.10/M tokens.
They're in the restaraurant business now, and don't know about you, but there are few restaurants good enough that I'll sit through rounds of forced ads while waiting for my food. Too much decent competition, I can also self-host food at home, and switching cost is low. The best restaurant in the world probably couldn't get away with such as business model. If they went $1T in debt to start a restaurant, they are probably never climbing out (no matter how good or automated the food is).

DanielHB [user?id=DanielHB] 1 day ago [item?id=50629344] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628771] | next [#50630271] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

People greatly underestimate switching costs, once these tools are embedded in your org it is hell. Sure it is not as bad as changing an ERP system, but in my org at least we have quite a integrations with Anthropic that would need to be rebuilt (or maybe the right term is reconfigured?) to use another provider. And that is on top of forcing users to switch from their existing UIs and workflows to different solutions.
Most orgs IT departments will happily keeping using Anthropic even if it is 10x more expensive unless the CFO personally comes knocking.

 [vote?id=50630522&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50630522]
program_whiz [user?id=program_whiz] 1 day ago [item?id=50630522] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50629344] | next [#50630271] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Yeah I'm sure there are some switching costs, but I'd put it closer to something like JIRA. Yes they can charge a premium and have some lockin for corporate. But this has a few effects:
1. Consumers don't care -- they use whatever (Todoist, etc)
2. There aren't network effects or heavy lock-in, its just momentum / inertia, so you're one bad quarter from fat-trimming
3. If the tool enshittifies people internally want to shift, and now they have AI to help them do it quickly and easily, plus save money.
4. You can't easily serve ads to people who are using the API (which was the whole point of the argument).
5. You can't justify trillion dollar valuations, you are at best a medium size SaaS company like Atlassian. They have $30B valuation, probably low for Anthropic given their ARR, but there is kind of a ceiling. You can't jack prices too much, and you aren't capturing "90% of global GDP", you are just one of many providers of a commodity.
6. You can't believe lock-in to an LLM is higher than lock-in to proprietary game engines, but companies switched when Unity tried to claw just a little value, and now Godot is a major player. Some tech is just a commodity and it isn't something you can build a moat from.
There is also kind of a ceiling on what companies will pay anyway (even though we're talking about ads), because what they care about is productivity and cost. If humans become cheaper, they will use them, so your ceiling for profit is whatever humans would charge for that labor and what your competitors charge (so the future where every company is paying $100k / month is unlikely).
When you say 10x you're talking about an extra $2k per employee per month, or an extra $24k per year. I think for most companies (especially small and medium), this is a major increase. For a 20 person org, saving $500k per year by switching a few lines of code is worth it. Sure, in FAANG land it won't matter, but they can host / use their own models and afford private on-staff chefs to cook lunch, so it isn't really representative.

pllbnk [user?id=pllbnk] 22 hours ago [item?id=50633757] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628643] | prev [#50631826] | next [#50629630] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

One thing I know really well about current LLMs is that they have terrible judgement, which is not surprising given that they are just token predictors. But "everybody's heads are spinning" because they see them type code really really quickly and then call commands that are able to test the typed code really really quickly too and then almost instantly rewrite their very very stupid mistakes until the code finally works. They are not one-shotting anything non-trivial, but many people think they are because the agentic harnesses provide a good facade and barely anyone reads intermediate results, as they only care about the final output.
But I digress. I think more people ought to try to delegate to agents real life decisions and they would see how idiotic the decisions they take care. Even when you specify them very clearly what you want, they will still come up with something stupid, because life doesn't have unit tests and the variables are endless.
So yeah, they can do targeted ads that will be about as effective as current generation of ad targeting and I doubt it could be more than that.

 [vote?id=50628844&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628844]
bryanlarsen [user?id=bryanlarsen] 1 day ago [item?id=50628844] | parent [#50628101] | prev [#50628643] | next [#50628401] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Anthropic is also at massive risk of bankruptcy.
Anthropic has 3 core beliefs:
- they are close to achieving recursive self-improvement (RSI)
- they have a moral imperative to be the first to reach recursive self-improvement
- once they hit recursive self-improvement, they'll kick off the singularity and that model will instantly make them insanely profitable
So they're obviously going to spend all their money on a Hail Mary pass to achieve RSI.
Mythos is an 8T model that cost about $10B and 1TWh. Let's assume that their RSI model is 2 generations of doubling away. Training cost is linear to size, but they'll probably also need to similarly increase training data, which is also linear. Each generation will be more efficient to train, but I'm confident the cost of assembling the training data will more than offset that efficiency gain.
So $160B to train that 32T model that kicks off the singularity. What then? Have the fixed the faulty business model that littlecranky67 demonstrates? No. Models still take billions of dollars to train. Chinese models are still 3-6 months behind. Smaller much cheaper models are still a better choice for most tasks.
They kicked off the singularity but cannot pay off the debt they took on to get there.
And that's the positive case. The negative case is that they spent $160B to train that model and don't achieve RSI...

reply [reply?id=50628844&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350628844]

 [vote?id=50629952&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50629952]
gen220 [user?id=gen220] 1 day ago [item?id=50629952] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628844] | next [#50629795] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I’m not an Anthropic apologist, but I don’t think this is an accurate representation of how they expect RSI takeoff to happen.
My understanding is that it’s a more gradual process. And that there’s no reason to expect a linear progression of parameter counts or training costs.
RSI is more about letting Mythos iterate on model architecture and training methods, which unlock efficiencies and performance improvements that don’t necessarily correlate with parameter counts.
Again I don’t have an opinion on whether this will or has already happened. Anthropic claims that they’re seeing evidence of it internally but it’s such a fuzzy target that it’s hard to judge without seeing a more capable model. But I do think it’s been clear that they have the $most_capable_model for almost a year now, and that others are playing catch up. That might be its own tell that they’re approaching RSI or not, but it’s impossible to know from the outside.

 [vote?id=50628248&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628248]
simias [user?id=simias] 1 day ago [item?id=50628248] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628132] | next [#50628163] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I think it will be a while, mainly because of how constrained the hardware is at the moment.
My main interrogation regarding Anthropic's business model at the moment is that it's not clear that even maintaining a healthy capability gap to the competition will be enough to justify the cost. In my opinion and experience, past a certain point you get fast diminishing returns. Most Claude users aren't using it to solve Millenium Prize problems.
If we eventually get a cheapo open-weight Chinese model that operates at the level of, say, Opus 5.5, won't that be Good Enough(tm) for 99% of use cases?
I think the counter-argument is that, as LLM capabilities improve, users get more ambitious and push the boundary of task complexity. I think it's probably true to some extent, but only to some extent.

People often say, open weight cheap models at the level of Opus are at the "Good Enough" frontier and therefore private models are doomed. But we are nowhere near the "Good Enough" frontier.
Not even Fable 5.2 is good enough for most tasks. As long as you look around and see seats filled with knowledge workers being paid 300k+/year, you know we simply are not at the good enough level. We are at the "Good tool in the hands of a capable person" level. It is like going back to antiquity and saying scythes were "Good Enough" at harvesting fields.

The point though, isn't whether open weight models could entirely replace 300k (or whatever) human workers.
Either frontier labs can, and open weight will follow suit a few months after.
Or frontier labs cannot. In which case open weight are or will become good enough.
The question is what moat can frontier labs, two of which values at over 1 trillion USD, keep long enough to build a valid business model upon.
It isn't even clear they have a moat strong enough to even keep needing businesses long enough to profit (see Revolut and plenty of others who decided open weight are better value already)

 [vote?id=50628351&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628351]
reticulates [user?id=reticulates] 1 day ago [item?id=50628351] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628163] | next [#50628202] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

No, Anthropic's gross margin on inference is not ~85%. A subset of tokens may have ~85% margins but the majority of their tokens are consumed by users on fixed price plans.
Anthropic had a very short period where their cash expenditure on compute was artificially low during which their margins looked good. If you look at their actual spend on compute, as described in their Q3 filings, it is barely above water. Anthropic spend over $5 billion per month on compute, their revenue barely exceeds their compute spend.
Economies of scale typically refers to margins increasing as scale increases but inference doesn't have that, inference is a fixed cost per token. More compute does not improve inference margins. Inference is becoming more efficient per unit of compute with various innovations but that is independent of access to compute (and that is bad for Anthropic given how much compute they have committed to).
Yes, inference can be profitable, nobody denies that, just charge more than the cost per token, but that isn't Anthropic's business model.

 [vote?id=50631875&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50631875]
willmadden [user?id=willmadden] 23 hours ago [item?id=50631875] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50630093] | next [#50628202] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

That's a KV cache limitation with the demo chip. They'll hardwire the model weights into ASICs, but keep the KV cache in stacked DRAM, and share memory across multiple ASICs. That solves it.
Did you see the tokens/second on that thing? It's unbelievably fast. Once the models stabilize and plateau a bit, it's the future.

reply [reply?id=50631875&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350631875]

 [vote?id=50644861&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50644861]
bigyabai [user?id=bigyabai] 38 minutes ago [item?id=50644861] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50631875] | next [#50628202] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It's an 8B model from 2 years ago, you can get unbelievable tok/s from TPUs too and not have to bake weights into SRAM.
I hate to be the bearer of bad news, but these ASICs will only be popular for non-LLM or tiny LLM applications. The ROI falls through the floor with larger models, and the reason we don't have anything more than a demo is that nobody wants to be the bagholder for something as useless as ChatJimmy.

I generally want to agree with you. However, as the Claude admin for a smaller organization I've been startled by the rate at which users are migrating their workflows into Claude chat.
Our developers remain fairly tool agnostic, but after only a few months I can say it will not be easy to migrate the rest of the organization out of Claude if we ever wanted to do so.
There is still a bit of friction when dealing with artifacts and collaboration. Users are generating word docs and excel and then sharing through the existing channels. However, looking at the feature flags in Claude admin is like watching their product strategy develop in real time. They are clearly trying to unseat Microsoft as the connective tissue of the organization, pulling more and more collaborative business tooling into the chat/cowork product.

Given their noise about biology and Anthropic's acquisitions in this space, a prediction from someone who works in drug development.
We all use Claude like crazy under business agreements. People belittle the AI-in-bio hype but something tangible will come out of that.

 [vote?id=50628269&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628269]
aurareturn [user?id=aurareturn] 1 day ago [item?id=50628269] | parent [#50628101] | prev [#50628314] | next [#50628378] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Our entire company have started using Claude in the last month. It started with a few early adopters, then something happened and everyone started using it. Employees are hooking up MCPs to it. They're absolutely raving about it.
Most are on the $20/month plan now. But increasingly, I'm seeing some employees hit the limit and request more tokens and higher tier plans.
No, we're not going to switch to an open source alternative because it's a bit cheaper. No, we're not going to suddenly switch to Gemini or ChatGPT.
Anthropic is building a strong moat in my opinion:
1. Model intelligence moat/training moat
2. Compute moat (all the cash and revenue flywheel means they can buy more compute than others)
3. Claude
I think people are highly underestimating #3. They're building spreadsheets, docs, and presentation into Claude as well. Once your company starts giving people Claude subscriptions, it's extremely hard to switch.
Edit: Funny enough, I wrote the exact same reasons 7 months ago here. At least I'm consistent in my view: https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=47670664 [https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=47670664]

 [vote?id=50628374&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628374]
simias [user?id=simias] 1 day ago [item?id=50628374] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628269] | next [#50628493] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

>No, we're not going to switch to an open source alternative because it's a bit cheaper. No, we're not going to suddenly switch to Gemini or ChatGPT.
Can you explain why not? I feel that's the crux of your argument.
You do have a point though, I think at the moment there's a "nobody gets fired for choosing Claude" mindset, where it's seen as the default and since things are moving so fast evaluating the cost and comparing the alternatives is extremely difficult.
But eventually things will settle. Setting aside a Singularity scenario where all of society is upended by the God Machine, there'll be a point when agent capabilities and development flows will standardize, the field will consolidate around a few key players/models and it'll become easier to shop around. At that point, I expect that margins will be crushed and it'll become difficult for companies to justify paying for Claude if you can get basically the same service for a fraction of the cost.
In many ways offering a pure inference SaSS product is easier than, say, creating a gmail competitor. Especially if hardware access becomes less constrained in a few years.

reply [reply?id=50628374&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350628374]

 [vote?id=50630479&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50630479]
orangea [user?id=orangea] 1 day ago [item?id=50630479] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628374] | next [#50630843] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I think that Claude is going for the same strategy that Dropbox tried and arguably failed: since the core product became a commodity, bundle in a bunch of productivity/ancillary features to differentiate the offering and introduce some friction to switching.
The argument that it might work this time is that non-technical users don't think in terms of concepts like cloneable API surfaces like the chat completions API, standard protocols like MCP servers, etc. Someone like me is perfectly happy to use some open source web chat UI and hook it up to OpenRouter. But people who don't know how this stuff works will worry that if they switch away from Claude then they will have to completely rethink how they do everything because they don't realize (to a high enough degree) that any competitor can just create more or less exactly the same thing.
Everyone understands that Box and Google Drive and Dropbox are basically interchangeable, so the question is: is there something unique about AI that lets providers escape this fate?

reply [reply?id=50630479&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350630479]

 [vote?id=50628493&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628493]
piva00 [user?id=piva00] 1 day ago [item?id=50628493] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628269] | prev [#50628374] | next [#50628702] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

You just started in this journey, the company I work for has been riding the AI/agent wave very close to the beginning of the hype. Experiments with GPT-3.5, general rollout of GPT 4o to 4.5 for devs, then to Claude 4 to 4.5, and since then we've had access to all frontier models. Right now we also have our own deployment of GLM-5.3 and a few other open weight models, running in our infra without paying tokens to an external party for simpler tasks.
There's no moat, we switch models on each release to assess which one is performing better for our tasks and people naturally gravitate to those until a new one appears, gets assessed and if it works better we just switch a toggle to use that one instead.
It's only hard to switch if your company makes it hard to switch, otherwise they're mostly a drop-in replacement.

 [vote?id=50628702&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628702]
time0ut [user?id=time0ut] 1 day ago [item?id=50628702] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628269] | prev [#50628493] | next [#50628331] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I am seeing the same. 2026 was all about onboarding our engineers. 2027 is looking like it will be about onboarding everyone else.
The ecosystem is developing. Once an org has the controls, connections, and processes built on one, it will be painful to switch.
Anthropic and OpenAI have been aggressively working to create connectivity into our industry. We have built and contributed MCP tools (connectors) and published to them and Microsoft (which is just hilariously complicated and painful in comparison). No demand for a Google equivalent btw.
On cost though, I think we are walking straight into a squeeze. I think engineers are relatively adaptable. We have switched harnesses and models over the past year a couple times and people are trying new things. I suspect our business users will not be so adaptable and our security and compliance teams even less so. The momentum will be really strong.
I recently worked on a little experiment at home that maxed my $20 Claude plan 4 weeks in a row. It was ~$1000 of usage if I was paying full API price. That is what is coming for their enterpise customers eventually.

 [vote?id=50631859&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50631859]
Tuna-Fish [user?id=Tuna-Fish] 23 hours ago [item?id=50631859] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50630385] | next [#50637209] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

There are competitors charging a lot less.
Kimi K3 is a huge (3T parameter) open weight model, the cheapest it is available from 3rd party providers is $0.25/1M (non-cached) input, $9/1M output.
This model is not as good as the ones Anthropic uses, but it's not dramatically cheaper to run.
Inference is very cheap. Anthropic and OpenAI are both trying desperately to maintain the perception of very high token costs, to sustain the more expensive parts of their businesses. Neither of them wants to cut the costs, but also they compete for customers by having these subscriptions, which are in line with the external estimates for their actual costs.

reply [reply?id=50631859&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350631859]

 [vote?id=50634027&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50634027]
sarchertech [user?id=sarchertech] 21 hours ago [item?id=50634027] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50631859] | next [#50637209] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

A 99% profit margin means you are charging 100x more than it costs you.
Opus 5.5 costs 8x more input and 2x more output than the open weight model you listed.
Assuming open weight and frontier inference have similar costs, to make your numbers work, the 3rd party compute sellers would currently need to be selling inference for 12x their costs. A 92% profit margin.
For someone running an open model and competing in a marketplace that is absurd.

There’s really not that much moat in LLMs. A Googler’s “we have no moat” memo still holds true today.
It is a commodity, after all; not a winner take all game. Even ignoring open source, if $LAB_A has a better model, then their model will happily help you import all your data from $LAB_B; even if it’s by the means of computer use.

 [vote?id=50630286&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50630286]
sarchertech [user?id=sarchertech] 1 day ago [item?id=50630286] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50630076] | next [#50628704] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

1. That’s a less of an attractive model if your gym only serves business people who use the equipment in your gym to do their jobs, and they all work the same 9-5 M-F hours.
2. Looking at the prices we’re paying, with 90% profit margin it wouldn’t take max usage just reasonably sustained usage.
3. A $20 subscription from every single office worker in the US at a generous 50% margin is less than 10 billion per year. That’s not even close to the kind of money they need.
They need to make something like $25 a month from ever single information worker on the planet to justify their valuation.
Thats profit. After paying for COGS and training and everything else.

 [vote?id=50629261&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50629261]
halfcat [user?id=halfcat] 1 day ago [item?id=50629261] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628481] | prev [#50628742] | next [#50628470] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> I don't think we can simply replace Claude like this anymore
You absolutely can. Copilot literally lets you pick Anthropic or OpenAI in the M365 Copilot Admin settings (and only OpenAI keeps your data from leaving Microsoft servers).
It’s almost like they’re interchangeable (because they are).
Every AI tool can work with spreadsheets, presentations, docs, and so on. Most of these “docs” are HTML files anyway, if Claude created it. But even if you’re working with Excel, every AI tool can read and write those.
MCP is literally a standard, has nothing to do with Claude specifically. Every AI ecosystem works with MCP.
There’s no moat in the products themselves. The only moat is in regulatory capture (at which point your $20/mo plan will be priced at a few thousand per month per seat).

halfcat [user?id=halfcat] 21 hours ago [item?id=50634209] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50632450] | next [#50628470] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I think about airlines, piracy, and cannabis. They don’t have to prevent it. They just have to make corporate use legally and financially risky or uncertain.
Businesses just pay the Microsoft Windows license fee instead of pirating it. Most people just pay for Netflix. Most people aren’t going to operate a dispensary when there’s an upfront investment cost, and the legal situation is on-again-off-again.
They don’t have to get widespread consensus either. Like how California passing a law can have a chilling effect everywhere, or GDPR in the EU caused websites everywhere to change.
I’m not saying I think they will achieve regulatory capture. They might. Or they might just go the way of the airline industry: vastly beneficial to society, without any single company able to capture most of the value.

That's not the comparison you should be making. You're comparing a corporate subscription to Claude to running your own models when the comparison should be to some other company hosting an open weight model and selling tokens when they, unlike Anthropic, have no training costs. Ultimately I believe the only factor that will matter is performance-per-Watt. Researchers believe the lead of frontier models is only 6-12 months. We already have things like OpenRouter that treat cloud LLMs as largely interchangeable.
The entire valuation of these 3 companies hinges on there being a moat and they themselves "winning" at AI. They can't all win and there is no model moat long term. There is still a hardware moat but that's temporary as China plays catch up with NVidia, which absolutely will happen. My current guess is by 2030 you'll see Chinese made AI chips in volume.

hodder [user?id=hodder] 1 day ago [item?id=50628192] | parent [#50628101] | prev [#50628449] | next [#50634228] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Total nonsense. The vast majority of fortune 500 companies who are the major customers are NOT spinning up qwen models for employees. Notice how fortune 500 companies do not use Libre Office and GIMP.
People on this site live in a bubble. There has never in history been a startup more successful at growing revenue than Anthropic. People sit on the sidelines and make these wild armchair quarterback calls, but Anthropic is executing.
Further, we are in VERY early innings of the LLM race, sure the models might appear commodified to some extent at the frontier today, but that does not extrapolate to the future well.
It is highly likely one model achieves a degree of RSI that competition fails to close. We may be there already. I dont see anyone using open weight models to solve frontier millennium math problems.
The chances that open weight models keep up over the next decade is extremely low. Costs are also simply too high, they will form a barrier to entry. Pre training costs are going to enter a regime where open models simply cannot compete. You will not have an open weight model with $500b pretraining costs. You will absolutely get there in private models.

 [vote?id=50628800&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628800]
aurareturn [user?id=aurareturn] 1 day ago [item?id=50628800] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628192] | next [#50628230] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

 The chances that open weight models keep up over the next decade is extremely low. Costs are also simply too high, they will form a barrier to entry. Pre training costs are going to enter a regime where open models simply cannot compete. You will not have an open weight model with $500b pretraining costs. You will absolutely get there in private models.

I agree with this. Below is what I wrote 6 months ago[0]:

 I think frontier models are a classic duopoly/monopoly scenario. The smarter the model, the more it gets used, the more revenue it generates, the more compute the company can buy, the smarter the next model and so on. It's a flywheel effect. This is similar to advanced chip nodes like TSMC where your current node has to make enough money to pay for the next node. TSMC owns something like 95%+ of all of the most advanced node market. Back in the 80s and 90s, you had dozens of chip fab companies. Today, there are only 3. There should only be 1 but national security saved Intel and Samsung fabs.

 There is evidence that the Chinese models are falling further behind, not gaining. Consolidation will likely happen soon because many unprofitable open source labs will have to merge and focus on revenue generation.

[0]https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48182917 [https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48182917]

 [vote?id=50628498&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628498]
hodder [user?id=hodder] 1 day ago [item?id=50628498] | root [#50628101] | parent [#50628230] | prev [#50628417] | next [#50628496] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

You don't need just one model to win to beat open weight from tearing down the economics. You simply need scale. It seems likely to me that you will have a runwaway model as compute infra becomes less constrained, but regardless, once pre training costs exceed the "ticket to play" level, it is highly likely the current frontier open weight models fall far behind the well capitalized players. If it weren't for the Chinese governmnent, we would already be there.

fortran77 [user?id=fortran77] 21 hours ago [item?id=50634228] | prev [#50628101] | next [#50631911] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

A quick search shows that the majority of US IPOs fall below their IPO price in the first 90 days.
If you're not getting pre-IPO shares, buying the stock on day 1 is almost always a bad idea, no matter what the company. Better off waiting a quarter or two and seeing how they're doing. There's plenty of time if the company will be successful in the long run.

 [vote?id=50629246&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50629246]
notenchange [user?id=notenchange] 1 day ago [item?id=50629246] | root [#50628229] | parent [#50629007] | next [#50632801] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I'm talking about complex coding tasks.
Deepseek is good at solving a very specific problem, but as soon as you need more "intelligence" from it (i.e. understanding where the pieces fall together with the existing code base or coming up with tight solutions to the problem), it normally fails silently.
What I mean by that, is that it gives you a correct answer, but that correct answer clearly doesn't take into account many things the Opus 5.5. takes. Like security, edge cases, proper architecture, etc.

 [vote?id=50628790&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50628790]
bob1029 [user?id=bob1029] 1 day ago [item?id=50628790] | prev [#50629488] | next [#50633454] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Ultrafast inference might be the end of Anthropic. They don't have a good strategy for this right now and the demand for it is currently skyrocketing. "Fast" models at factors like 2.5x faster are not in the same category. You need a fundamentally different hardware architecture to support speeds beyond this.
If you haven't tried one of these models you need to. It's genuinely like broadband internet vs dialup. I can't go back anymore. It's too slow. I'm addicted now. Multiply this by everyone else on earth who will eventually try it.
6x cost per token sounds crazy until you actually feel the difference. It changes the ways you can work. Much like how broadband altered the business models of blockbuster and Netflix.
I think faster tokens might also mean we paradoxically use fewer because the human can be in the loop the whole time. We don't need speculation going on while we sleep.

PaulRobinson [user?id=PaulRobinson] 1 day ago [item?id=50629981] | parent [#50629358] | next [#50629434] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I think the writing has been on the wall for a while. In February I had a bet with a friend that OpenAI would "fail"[1] by end of the year. It seems, to rephrase a well-known idiom, that the market is staying irrational longer than my bet can stay solvent.
With Anthropic, the numbers never made much sense to me: the float produced a relatively small amount of money compared to projected operating losses. I think this was an exit liquidity event timed for private investors to get out before it was too late. I'm not saying that's a bad thing, it's just not very encouraging for the long-term fiscal picture of the company, or the sector.
I want to underline what I'm talking about here is the economic model of these businesses, not the technical, operational or value model. LLMs clearly have uses, they clearly help some people in some roles, plenty. OpenAI and Anthropic have produced magical technologies, compared to what existed before.
What's not clear is that they do that in a way that is going to sustain current valuations and analyst projections.
I'm old enough to have worked through the dotcom crash. This feels eerily similar, and I think it'll play out the same: the tech will have durable value that persists, but not at the stock market valuations placed on it by people who don't understand it.
[1] Failure was defined as the usual modes (Chapter 11, etc.), but also unable to make payroll, or a government taking a substantial shareholding which I'd interpret as a state-sanctioned bailout. I'm actually surprised it didn't land, but I might offer a double-or-nothing for 2027.

>When I lose a bet, I will admit defeat, pay promptly, and hold my tongue – never protesting that I was “really right.”
From The Bettor's Oath. [0]
Bravo for making a bet instead of casually predicting, but never miss a chance to look good by accepting the missed prediction (if it does come to pass you lost).
[0] (https://www.econlib.org/archives/2012/05/the_bettors_oat.htm... [https://www.econlib.org/archives/2012/05/the_bettors_oat.html]).

I think on a small or large scale there is a simple-minded reckoning that's likely to come up in some situations sooner or later.
The feeling comes over people that with so much money invested by this point, if that's not enough to prosper on going forward, then what good is more money going to do anyway?
Even if perfection has not been achieved, "just" go ahead and leverage the huge pile you've got already. Keep in mind there are business people who could amaze you with what they could accomplish with way less invested, not just without AGI but with no AI at all.

ben_w [user?id=ben_w] 20 hours ago [item?id=50634924] | root [#50629358] | parent [#50629949] | prev [#50630016] | next [#50630060] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> The real question is not even how much of the pie AI is going to gobble, it’s whether this particular player is going to do it and not someone else.
The beneficiaries may be nobody in this industry, just the users of the tech.
Wikipedia is not valued at {peak sale price of Encyclopedia Britannica} * {number of people on the internet}, which from memory would be somewhere around 14-18 trillion USD.
Competition can drive prices down; free models could drive it down a lot if progress ever does stall, which everyone expects eventually (at least if the people understand physics; even the singularity needs to care about physics).
Not even the people making the hardware have a sure place, both because hardware is still getting better per unit cost, and because models are getting their capabilities to fit on smaller hardware.

marginalia_nu [user?id=marginalia_nu] 1 day ago [item?id=50629940] | root [#50629358] | parent [#50629434] | prev [#50630308] | next [#50630814] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Eh there are good reasons to expect this wouldn't take off like a rocket if you scrutinize the IPO a bit.
Their IPO filing was itself pretty suspect, conspicuously missing Q3 2026 financial results and data center lease commitments. Not exactly filling investors to the brim with confidence.
Then they IPO:ed without a traditional 180 day lock up period[1], meaning insiders were able to sell ~$200 million worth of shares day one (up to 10% of what they own). This isn't illegal or unprecedented, but rare and pretty questionable, and with a general whaft of wishing to do a quick pump and dump rather than something you do out of long term confidence. As an insider you'd be an absolute moron not to dump those 10% at first opportunity to first best willing bag holder to secure money in hand as you still sit on the remaining 90% if the IPO performs well.
Morgan Stanley were underwriters and supposed to stabilize the IPO but seems to have blown their entire load on day one[2]. Possibly buying up those insider shares, greenshoe agreement covered only about $150 million.
All of this creates a downward thrust for the stock. That's excluding the ambient questions regarding the dem landslide and probable data center delays and financing, the circus around huang and nvidia.
[1] https://finance.yahoo.com/markets/stocks/articles/anthropic-... [https://finance.yahoo.com/markets/stocks/articles/anthropic-lock-calendar-ant-insiders-000000339.html]
[2] https://finance.yahoo.com/markets/stocks/articles/anthropic-... [https://finance.yahoo.com/markets/stocks/articles/anthropic-ipo-safety-net-gone-095030591.html]

uptrails [user?id=uptrails] 1 day ago [item?id=50629658] | root [#50629358] | parent [#50629448] | prev [#50630256] | next [#50630082] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

A company with high P/E can be, quite rationally, valued more fairly than a company with low P/E if the first one has very uncertain outlook for future revenues, while the second one is a boring, stable business that isn't going anywhere.
Anthropic is a perfect example of the former. Their current revenue numbers, or their current growth rate, tell me nothing useful about their revenue 2-3 years from now.
