 [vote?id=50629619&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50629619]
cmiles8 [user?id=cmiles8] 1 day ago [item?id=50629619] | root [#50629358] | parent [#50629448] | prev [#50630387] | next [#50630239] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The market is more reacting to:
1. The fact that they will still need to raise more cash and reports saying they’ll get a “junk” bond rating, combined with interest rates going up that’s an ugly setup
2. The NPV of foundation models trending towards zero, with limited clarity on how they’ll make money in that future reality. The main money to be made is in selling compute but they primary rent that from others.
3. The coming ROI crisis where companies are scaling back AI efforts, raising questions on if future commitments are now too much. They’re pushing corporate customers hard but those corporates are all A) increasingly using model routers to send inference to the cheapest bidder, and B) rolling back AI budgets. Both those are bear signals for ANT.
4. Jensen coming back which is generally bad for them. He’s feeding the open model ecosystem that challenges Anthropic’s existence. The regulatory capture games this fall didn’t pan out as the AI bros had hoped.
The numbers in isolation are one thing, but with the full context there’s not a lot holding the price up and the market is realizing AI is likely way over-valued. This is all typical boom/bust stuff and we seem to be entering “bust” phase now.

throw0101c [user?id=throw0101c] 1 day ago [item?id=50629687] | parent [#50629358] | prev [#50629448] | next [#50630040] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> There aren’t many/any fundamentals keeping the stock price anywhere close to these levels.
Anthropic is no different than any other IPO, most of end up lower than their opening price. There's plenty of research on this:
> We’ve previously compared IPOs to lotteries that are prone to inflated valuations and low returns. Today we welcome “Mr. IPO,” Professor Jay Ritter onto the show for a deeper dive into IPO performance, for his insights into SPACs, and to hear his research into why economic growth doesn’t correlate with stock returns. Early in the episode, Jay unpacks how long-term IPO returns perform against first-day trading. While exploring the role that venture capital plays in tech IPOs, Jay talks about why negative earnings don’t affect tech IPOs in the short-term before sharing how skewness factors tend to impact young companies. Reflecting on how IPOs are usually underpriced, Jay discusses how the interests of companies are not aligned with the interests of IPO underwriters. After looking into IPO allocation, Jay compares the 2020 ‘hot IPO market’ with the internet bubble of the late 90s. Later, we ask Jay about what special-purpose acquisition companies (SPACs) are and why they’ve exploded in recent years. His answers highlight their investing benefits, risks, and why SPACs might be a better option for companies than IPOs. We examine how SPACs have historically performed and then jump into our next topic; why economic growth isn’t a good indicator that a country is worth investing in. He touches on why returns don’t correlate with economic growth, the place of capital gains and dividend yields when investing abroad, and how innovations in an industry can lead to higher stock returns. We wrap up our conversation by asking Jay for his take on whether the stock market is efficient before hearing how he defines success in his life. Tune in to hear our incredible and informative talk with Jay Ritter.
* https://rationalreminder.ca/podcast/139 [https://rationalreminder.ca/podcast/139]
* https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Ritter [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Ritter]
* https://warrington.ufl.edu/directory/jay-ritter/ [https://warrington.ufl.edu/directory/jay-ritter/]

jrflo [user?id=jrflo] 1 day ago [item?id=50630026] | parent [#50629358] | prev [#50629917] | next [#50630650] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Stock prices are quite literally a function of hype, hopes, and dreams; i.e. expectations of future value, not present value. If you think the market is "irrational" or "departing from fundamentals", you actually just don't understand the market, because the market is effectively the most efficient representation of the current information landscape.
If you do actually know better than the market, you better get to Wall St. because you'll quickly become a billionaire.

zug_zug [user?id=zug_zug] 1 day ago [item?id=50629984] | prev [#50633991] | next [#50631265] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

There are two types of investors. Professionals and novices. Novices says "Technology X sounds like the future, therefore I'll pick some company that I hear a lot about that does technology X and buy that stock, no matter what it costs right now."
The professionals actually read all of the financials of the company and can say for any company "This company is a buy at a certain price, and that price I estimate to be $Z."
People who are not in the professional should probably stick to index funds, and holding for years at a time, because (respectfully) you're the sucker at the poker table.

reply [reply?id=50629984&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350629984]

Not at all.
In the modern day, there 4 types of active investors.
1 Big firms tied to wallstreet with some form of insider knowledge who can basically do market manipulation at this point.
2 The retail "smart" inverters who try to follow logic and are most allways on the opposite end of the trades from group 1
3 The /r/wallstreetbets gamblers that invest on pure memes.
If you wanna make money, do literally the opposite of what makes sense. Anthropic is gonna go high. Wall street knows that AI sentiment is there, and as a result, its gonna let the price go down. Then they are gonna buy up a lot of it, generate some hype (watch Anthropic get handed a big government contract), and its gonna go up, then they will dump all of it.

 [vote?id=50634289&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836#50634289]
IAmGraydon [user?id=IAmGraydon] 21 hours ago [item?id=50634289] | prev [#50632990] | next [#50629858] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

All this talk about valuations is so misguided. Stocks don't trade on true, rational valuations anymore, and they haven't since sometime around 2020. They trade on vibes - how much mindshare they take up in the public's mindspace combined with the general brand/feel the public has for them. Why do you think NVidia's market share jumped $1 trillion just on the news that their well-liked and personable CEO would return, even though he clearly makes very questionable business and personal decisions and may be legally compromised?
Here's the thing - no one really likes Dario - he comes off like a crazed, power-obsessed nerd. No one really likes Anthropic. No one really likes the fear mongering while working on technology that they claim will take everyone's jobs. No one really wants them to succeed. Anthropic is not a vibes machine. Therefore, they will be punished on the markets.

reply [reply?id=50634289&goto=item%3Fid%3D50627836%2350634289]
